The entire oval is bordered by a green Ouroboros (a serpent biting its own tail). Faintly printed in a circular arrangement around the central emblem is the title text.</image-desc>

## Hermes. <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary Hellenistic figure regarded as the founder of alchemy and the "Hermetic" arts.</note>

*In the upper sphere, in the middle of the fountain, is a vein, which is the first rule of the philosophers.* <note>original Latin: "In superiori Sphæra est in medio fontis vena, quæ est Philosophorum Regula prima." In alchemical imagery, the "fountain" often represents the source of the universal solvent or "mercurial water" used to break down substances.</note>

## Arros. <note>An authority often cited in early alchemical texts, sometimes identified as a king or an early practitioner in the Arabic alchemical tradition.</note>

*All metals, in terms of their purity, are of the same substance: they differ only in their impurity, and by a greater or lesser digestion.* <note>original Latin: "Metalla omnia quoad puritatem sunt ejusdem substantiæ: differunt tantum impuritate, majori vel minori digestione." Alchemists believed that all metals were "growing" in the earth toward the perfection of gold; "digestion" refers to this natural ripening process driven by the earth's internal heat.</note>

# Preface.

The Book of Grace <note>original: "Gnadenbuch"; refers to the Holy Scriptures or revealed Word of God</note> stands clearly before our eyes; yet, Almighty God has also written such an image into the Book of Nature—that is, the heavens and the earth and everything within them—so that these two books agree with one another, as is publicly evident today. How then can Nature, as the most beautiful creation, be contrary to God the Lord, in which the Creator takes such great pleasure and through which He still works daily according to His good pleasure? It is therefore quite possible for the heathens <note>original: "Heyden"; referring to non-Christians or those without access to scripture</note> to come to the Book of Grace from this natural book through God's blessing, as, beyond all doubt, the <term>Philosophi Magi</term> <note>The "Philosopher-Magi" or Wise Men; here referring to the biblical Magi or ancient natural philosophers who sought divine truth through the stars and nature</note> or Wise

Paul says in 1 Corinthians 3: I have planted, Apollos has watered, but God gives the growth <note>original: "Gedeÿen." Refers to the successful outcome or flourishing of a project.</note>.

Alanus the Philosopher <note>Likely referring to Alain de Lille, a 12th-century scholar and mystic.</note>: Son, set your heart more toward God than toward the Art <term>Art: The "Great Art" or Alchemy, the practice of transforming matter and spirit.</term>, for it is a gift from God; and to whom He wills, He shares it. # The Opened Cabinet of Nature
<margin>Signature: e</margin>

> "When God sees that you are of an upright and well-meaning spirit, He will, out of His grace, allow you to master this Art <note>The 'Art' refers to Alchemy, specifically the creation of the Philosopher's Stone</note>. Denis Zachaire <note>original: "Dionyf. Zach." - a 16th-century French alchemist</note>, page 69. It is fitting for you that you first read the Philosophers with patience, constancy, and tireless effort before you stretch out your hand to the work. Always ask God for His grace and understanding, for no one comes to this Art through luck or without danger; rather, it is achieved through prayer more than through any other means—yet, means <note>physical materials and laboratory processes</note> must still be employed." The great King and Philosopher Hermes <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary founder of alchemy</note> in his book *On the Unity of Being* <note>original Latin: "de unitate entis"</note>: "Direct yourself entirely toward that which is above you; lift the wings of your understanding to the radiance of the higher substance. Then you shall behold with your eyes, both inwardly and outwardly, those countless and high beauties and another Light which surpasses all lights. You will be filled with wonder and will hold all the works of the world in no regard. You will even wish for and choose death in your heart before the proper time, and you will chastise <note>original: "casteyen"</note> and mortify your body. Beyond this, however, you will deny and hate your own soul, praising the King of all kings and the God of Glory with heartfelt and beautiful songs of praise. And the Word of the Father, which has loved us so much and is the Father's heart, we shall worship and love with all our heart and soul, marvelling at such a great treasure..."

<summary>This page features citations from Denis Zachaire and Hermes Trismegistus emphasizing that alchemical success requires a pure heart, constant prayer, and spiritual transcendence rather than mere physical effort. It describes the ecstatic state of the practitioner who glimpses the divine light and devalues worldly existence in favor of spiritual union.</summary>

# The Opened Cabinet of Nature

Whoever does not understand the metallic growth is already far removed from our Art, for he has no firm root upon which he may found and establish the certainty of his undertaking. This is also confirmed by the excellent philosopher Arnold of Villanova <note>Arnaldus de Villa Nova (c. 1240–1311), a famous physician and alchemist</note> in the following words:
> Whoever does not understand or know the recounted and naturally composed root of minerals <note>original: "mineralium"</note>, he also does not understand the initial and natural beginning of their growth; it follows from this that he knows the Art even less.

As the excellent Magus and philosopher Aristotle the Chemist <note>A pseudo-Aristotelian authority often cited in medieval alchemical texts</note> also speaks thus: that such a person will necessarily not attain the final goal of these matters. These profound admonitions of highly wise people serve our intent and purpose right well. For he speaks there of the matter of the blessed stone <note>The Philosopher's Stone</note>, as the *Rosarium Major* <note>The "Rosarium Philosophorum," a landmark 16th-century alchemical treatise</note> explains on page 219:
☞ <note>The original manuscript features a manicule (pointing hand) here to emphasize the text</note>
> namely, that all error originates from this: because the right ground and origin of the true matter of the Stone <note>original: "Lapidis"</note> is hidden and sealed from them. And it follows from this (he says further) that he who does not know the right beginning will never attain his desired end.

For he who does not know what he seeks, also does not know what he will find: therefore, all those who seek without a true foundation must...

# The Opened Cabinet of Nature

*Hermes, King and Philosopher* <note>original: "Hermes Rex & Philosophus"; referring to Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary founder of alchemy</note>, says that no foreign thing which has not originated from or been composed of *Metallic Matter* <note>original: "Metallischen Materia"</note> has the power to produce metals, to change them, or to *transmute* <note>transform one substance into another</note> them. *Count Bernhard* <note>Bernhard Trevisan, a 15th-century alchemist</note> *on page 20* also testifies to this: "Every substance has primarily its own specific seed from which it breaks forth, and the same is increased through that seed alone, and through no other." And he adds on *page 21* that the same is born and increased through the metals. But before I *treat* <note>original: "tractire"</note> this point together with the *requisites* or associated matters, I consider it good that I first undertake the entire *series generally* <note>original: "seriem generaliter"</note>, and thereafter each *specifically* <note>original: "in specie"</note> according to the list which I have committed myself to describe; for *from generalities*, the *specialties* <note>original: "ex generalibus... specialia"</note> can be more easily recognized and observed.

However, in the description of this high art and Divine Wisdom—which is rightly a secret of natural *Philosophy*—one must primarily observe and consider the *subject*, the *matter*, or the *unclean Chaos* <margin>☞</margin> <note>original: "Chaos impurum"</note> as the *Philosophers* call it. This is that which Nature has brought into a metallic form within the earth but has left imperfect; the artist must gradually purify this same thing without [excessive] heat. For as soon as the external warmth...

# The Great Peasant

For otherwise, the metallic body <note>original: "Metallisch corpus"</note> cannot be easily dissolved <note>original: "resolvirt"</note> and unlocked because of the impurity that comes to it from a natural defect <note>original: "ex vitio naturali"</note>. For the metallic form <note>original: "forma metallica"</note> must be removed, and this occurs through gentle boiling and digestion, dissolving <note>original: "solviren"</note> and coagulating <note>original: "coaguliren"</note>, so that you may bring it to a pure quintessence <note>original: "qvinta essentia"; the "fifth element" or purest concentrated essence</note>, a clear mercurial water, or a crystalline salt of metals <note>original: "Crystallinischen sale metallorum"</note>. This is nothing other than a pure, clean sulfur <note>original: "Sulphur"</note> which does not burn, for the reason that it is only pure natural heat into which natural moisture has been poured. 

For the gentle, prepared fire has this nature and property: it joins the parts that belong together even better and more densely through the smallest parts <note>original: "per minima"</note>—that is, it mixes <note>original: "permiscirt"</note> and binds the waters with waters inseparably in the most internal way. It further destroys those things which are antagonistic and consumes them entirely, transmuting <note>original: "transmutiret"</note> and changing them into a worthless ash. Therefore, the philosophers <note>original: "philosophi"</note> desire that one should bring the outermost to the innermost, and the innermost to the outermost; then one has the Art. This means that one should, through the special skill of the artist, clear away the coarse, earthy, combustible, sulfurous parts which appear in the outermost part of the matter. other, except that they have a difference in this regard: namely, as far as their purification is concerned, and their <term>Digestion</term> <note>the process of maturing or "cooking" a substance with gentle heat</note>. And although this seems wondrous, because lead is an impure metal while silver is a beautiful, delicate, and pure **metal**, let no one be persuaded otherwise; for Nature casts out the impurity of the lead over the length of time, as can be proven. # The Opened Cabinet of Nature

whether it may be of the Moon <note>original: "Luna"; silver</note> or of the Sun <note>original: "Sole"; gold</note>, as shall hereafter be proven.

But that all metals sprout from a single root, the true philosophers who have possessed the Stone <note>original: "Lapidem"; the Philosopher's Stone</note> write thus: One must indeed give credit to an artist or craftsman in what he speaks concerning the craft he has learned; I say nothing of such high masters, the Magi <note>original: "Magorum"</note> and Philosophers. King Geber <note>An 8th-century alchemist often cited as the father of chemistry</note> and the *Clangour of the Trumpet* <note>original: "Clangor Bucc."; an influential alchemical text</note> in the *Assembly of Philosophers* <note>original: "turba"</note> page 473, speak quite usefully on this: *According to the variety of the sulfur and its multiplication, diverse metals are procreated in the earth* <note>original Latin: "secundum varietatem Sulphuris & ipsius multiplicationem diversa metalla procreantur in terra"</note>. That is, <margin>25. 26. B.</margin> according to the distinction and abundance of the sulfur, and also its increase, different metals are born within the earth. But:

> Their first matter <note>original: "materia"</note> from which they became is one and the same; for the metals do not differ from one another except by way of accidental effects. That is, one has received a greater or lesser, a more tempered or untempered heat, or a combustible or incombustible sulfur within the belly of the earth.

Therefore (he says), the host of philosophers agree; how that comes to pass will follow hereafter in the section on how metals are composed by Nature, or from what they are made. unclean and difficult to dissolve. Therefore, the
philosophers have chosen the nearest and easiest way
and taken the material that can most easily

<term>generative and multiplicative power</term><note>vis generativa uñ multiplicativa</note>

reach the conclusion—especially since the material

<margin>☛</margin> plague myself for one or two years in the <term>dissolution</term><note>Resolution; the process of breaking a substance down into its primary components</note>?

<summary>The author explains that while the Philosophers' Stone can theoretically be made from any metal ore, the vital 'coloring spirit' is lost if the ore is smelted. He argues for choosing materials that are easily dissolved and still contain their primordial generative power to save time in the alchemical process.</summary>

# The Opened Cabinet of Nature

Mercury <note>The alchemical "Mercurius," referring here not to common quicksilver but to the "philosophical mercury" or the living spirit of metals</note> is not just any substance, but that in which Nature has ended her first operation and placed into a metallic nature, yet left it imperfect. The same author on page 394: this alone, because it is metallic, contains within itself what is necessary for the <margin>☞</margin> Work. *The Sound of the Trumpet* <note>original: "Clangor Buccinæ," a famous alchemical treatise</note> page 476.

> » Epimidius the Philosopher says in the *Assembly of the Philosophers* <note>original: "Turba [Philosophorum]"</note> that through a tempered heat it is extracted from the matter; that is, through a gentle warmth, a slimy or vaporous sliminess is drawn out of a metallic matter. It is, however, subtle; this is the **Sublimation of the Philosophers**. As Hermes <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary founder of alchemy</note> says: "make the thick subtle and return this to a thick state" <note>original: "craſſum fac ſubtile & hoc ſpiſſum reddito"</note>; an earthiness mixed and well-purified. This they call the **Elixir** (that is, a general medicine for humans, animals, trees, metals, and herbs) through which one transforms the metals.

It would be too long to set down all the authorities and proofs here; I will set down several locations here that you can read and look up for yourselves: Arnoldus in the *Rosary* <note>Arnold of Villanova's "Rosarium Philosophorum"</note> page 405; Flamel page 137; *The Sound of the Trumpet* page 475 and page 510; *Let us receive* <note>original: "Recipiamus"</note> Flamel page 141; the section on the Stone <note>original: "§. Lapis"</note> in the *Assembly* page 155 and page 433; *but it must be known* <note>original: "ſed ſciendum"</note>; Bernard, Count of Trevisan page 21 and page 35. These are thus enough so that one does not doubt that the matter of the Stone is or has a metallic form or shape; but this must be taken from it through the **Solution** <note>The process of dissolving a solid into a liquid state, the first step of the Great Work</note>, as will follow hereafter. Because it is a metallic body... <margin>☞</margin>

# The Opened Cabinet of Nature

> "It is quite possible that they might set aside all other arts of **Alchemy** <note>original: "Alchymi"</note> and use only that which can be accomplished in me and with me. The **Stone of Cold** is in me: that is, my water, with which I am able to congeal and freeze the spirits of the six metals into the bodily essence of the seventh—that is, to <term>promote</term> <note>To advance or refine a substance to a higher state.</note> gold with silver." The Dialogue of Philosophy explains this on page 14, 16: one must not take the source from which the metals originated. No: rather, one must take that which is extracted from the metals, and it is precisely that which lies hidden within the metals. This is explained quite elegantly in the *Turba Philosophorum* <note>original: "Turba", an influential early alchemical assembly text</note> part 1, page 577, which provides an example of a tree. If one wishes to grow a tree, one must not take water or earth, but rather that which comes from the tree itself, such as the seed or a small graft; this is then entrusted back to Nature. # The Great Peasant

Thus you have, my dear friend, the instruction not only regarding the <term>concordances</term> <note>The points of agreement or harmony between different authors</note> of the philosophers, but also concerning the materials. May the eternal, almighty God grant that you may use it for His honor and for the benefit of your neighbor.

How one should proceed further will follow in the second part. This treasure cannot be paid for with any gold, as Solomon says in his Wisdom in the 7th chapter, and silver is to be counted as mere sand compared to it. <note>A reference to Wisdom of Solomon 7:9: "All gold in her sight is but a little sand, and silver shall be counted as clay before her."</note>

Furthermore, other things concerning the <term>Gur</term> <note>A term in early modern alchemy referring to a primordial, metallic "juice" or earthy vapor found in mines, believed to be the precursor to metals</note> and the <term>first matter</term> <note>original: "prima materia"; the primitive, formless base substance required for the alchemical Great Work</note> will be shown; and there is absolutely no doubt, for I would wish to have seen and experienced so much that I could (if it were possible) also prove it with deeds; but it cannot be. Thus you have, my dear friend, the complete treatise of the entire matter, in which all philosophers agree.

It would have been somewhat shorter, but because I did not know whether the <term>heir</term> <note>original: "hæres"; the successor or recipient of this knowledge</note> knows Latin or not, I have set it down twice, albeit with difficulty. My son, simply look up the books of the philosophers; you will find it no different, although I can now prove the <term>entire operation</term> <note>original: "totam operationem"</note> with deeds, God be praised; and in this point it is best that one [finds] the <term>concordant</term> <note>original: "concordantes"; agreeing or harmonious</note> philosophers with one another and [in] one...

# The Opened Cabinet of Nature

And by its work, immense riches have resulted. For it is an inexhaustible treasure for men; those who use it enter into friendship with God, being commended for the gifts of learning. For it is a certain Breath of divine power, and a pure Outflowing of the Almighty, etc. <note>original Latin: "& ejus operâ immenſæ divitiæ evenerunt... Eſt enim Vapor quidam divinæ potentiæ." ->After the Clouds, the Sun.<- <note>original Latin: "Post Nubila Phoebus." A common motto suggesting that relief follows a period of hardship.</note>

" There was once an old saying or proverb:
" after great suffering, there usually comes
great joy. <note>original: "proverbium"</note> And so it is also the other way around. Thus,
unfortunately, it happened to me as well a few years ago: it
has likely happened to others too, who, when
they first began without a true basis or foundation,
fared almost exactly as I shall recount at
length. For when I believed I had the whole
world in my hand, I obtained less than nothing.
Since my glass—in which I had placed my entire
well-being <note>original: "Wolfahrt"</note>—shattered with a great bang,
the material splashed my **silent philosophers** <note>original: "mutos Philoſophos," a common alchemical term for books, which contain the wisdom of the masters but cannot speak aloud.</note> and books
here and there, to my great loss and disadvantage,
of which I must now refrain from mentioning further,
except that I was greatly frozen, silenced,
and terrified by this unexpected misfortune;
so much so, that out of mourning and grief I did not
know where I was, what I had intended, or how I should
proceed further.

<summary>The author reflects on a catastrophic alchemical failure where his equipment shattered, destroying his materials and damaging his library. He uses this personal misfortune to illustrate the proverb that joy and sorrow often follow one another in the pursuit of natural secrets.</summary>

Therefore, take <margin>6. ☞</margin> these two lilies, completely pure and polished / and <margin>9.</margin> ,, shut them very tightly in a crystal without <margin>1.</margin> ,, fire / place them in a gentle steam bath <note>original: "Schweißbad," literally a "sweat-bath" or low-heat balneum</note> / then the white lily will soon spread out wide / and take the red one into itself and enclose it / but because the red <margin>7.</margin> ,, one is of a more fiery nature / and finds help from the external warmth / it lets its hot balsamic scent out into the coldness of the white one / whereby they become at odds / for neither will yield <note>original: "cedirn"</note> or give way to the other / this is caused by their contrary qualities <note>original: "contrariæ qvalitates"</note>, as you well know / both grow up into the heavens / but are [carried] by the wind...

<meta>The author continues his narrative regarding his alchemical experiments and the social friction they caused in his community.</meta>

# The Unlocked Cabinet of Nature

I gathered a considerable supply of coal (for this work consumes a great deal of it) and built suitable <term>furnos</term> <note>Latin: furnaces</note> and ovens for this purpose. In a short time—within a few weeks—I had prepared a significant store. The Enemy of Christians <note>A common reference to the Devil or spiritual opposition</note> could not suffer this; he incited one uproar after another in all the streets. My neighbors complained about me, fearing I would set their houses on fire. My friends and other acquaintances reproached me, saying that counterfeit coins were currently circulating and "on the tracks" <note>A metaphor for being under investigation by authorities</note>. They urged me to desist from such vain pursuits so that I would not fall under suspicion. They insisted I should instead earnestly practice and attend to my <term>facultatem Juridicam</term> <note>Latin: legal profession</note>, as I was a Doctor of Laws, and could otherwise easily earn an abundant livelihood. 

However, I suffered great doubt as to whether I could earn my bread with a clear conscience through that profession. 
<margin>☞</margin>
The next trouble was that the common blacksmiths and goldsmiths denounced me before the City Council. They claimed I was making coal too expensive for them, and that they could no longer maintain their trades or civic livelihoods. Consequently <note>original: "per conseqvẽtiã"</note>, they argued they would be unable to pay their rents or <term>Schoß</term> <note>A medieval property tax or communal levy</note> to the Council and the city. This was because I paid a higher price for the coal so that I might secure it before they did. The curiosity of the common crowd <note>original: "curio vulgi"</note> occupies itself with gold, antimony <term>Spießglas: an early name for the mineral stibnite, used to produce antimony</term>, cinnabar ore, and many other lesser, trivial things. Furthermore, such a high gift <note>referring to the "Donum Dei" or the gift of alchemical knowledge</note> is not granted to everyone; therefore, every man should take account and examine himself well before loss and ruin overwhelm and creep up on him. Let him who can take note, take note: <margin>☞</margin> Now, when I had completed the course of my travels according to my fated appointment <note>original: "cursum itinerum secundum fatalem constitutionem"</note>, I returned home joyfully. There, my supposed friends appeared once more, wanting to know where I had been so long, what I had accomplished, and what I intended to do next. I gave them a brief answer, saying: "Is the world not large enough? You perhaps imagine your city is the entire world, and that one cannot sustain oneself outside of it. Oh no! God be praised, there are plenty of other people who recognize and accept with great gratitude that which you despise and mock. You should also know that I will not trouble you much now with the handling <note>original: "tractation"</note> of coals." <note>A metaphor for the dirty, laborious work of "charcoal burners" or false alchemists who focus only on the furnace rather than the philosophy.</note>

From this it now follows that all things which have a lasting substance, and which are intended to be and remain good, must—*according to the likeness* <note>original: "ad similitudinem"</note>—flow forth from one thing and be divided into three; and conversely, those three must be composed into one. Otherwise, it goes against the indication of the Most High, and nothing useful comes of it. These three, however, are properly Body, Soul, and Spirit—or Heavenly, Earthly, and Watery; *Salt, Sulfur, and Mercury* <note>The three prime principles of alchemy, representing stability, combustibility, and fluidity.</note>. And yet these three are properly one and together in a single thing or *subject* <note>original: "subjectis"</note>. Just as in Man there is Body, Soul, and Spirit; as in God there is Father, Son, and Holy Spirit; so also in all creatures there is Father, Mother, and Child. To confirm this, God the Righteous and Faithful—in order to prove His will and how everything should be—created Adam, His first son, in His likeness and image. And this single human was God's son and image *respectively* <note>original: "respectivè"</note>, namely Adam and not Eve; and the entire human race [was contained in him] and not in Eve. Thus, from the single Adam and Son of God, three things came to be: Father, Mother, and Child—

<margin>1.</margin> In the presence of the Most High, they obtained through prayer this high medicine known as the *Tincture of the Philosophers* <note>original: "tincturam Philosophorum"; a legendary alchemical substance capable of curing all ills and perfecting metals</note> for the preservation of long life and the resistance of all diseases. But such a claim is easily refuted if one first considers and compares the original *generation* <note>original: "generation"; the process of creation or birth</note> of man and of metals. Man was not created by God the Almighty out of a poor, impure, or common clod of earth—as the unlearned *theologians* <note>original: "Theologi"</note> and loud-mouths believe—but rather from <margin>10.</margin> the very best and most subtle extract, etc., which existed in the *entire center of the earth*. And believe me, for such a work—which the Almighty created in His own *image* <note>original: "Ebenbild"</note>, and into which He also placed, breathed, and implanted a small spark or ray of His eternal *essence* <note>original: "Essenz"</note> and being—

# CABALISTIC FIGURE

<margin>

[The page is dominated by a complex woodcut illustration.]

At the summit of the image, a radiant sun shines, casting a dense curtain of vertical rays that descend to form an oval, egg-like boundary. This <term>Cosmic Egg</term> <note>A symbol of the universe in its potential state, often used in alchemy to represent the sealed vessel where the "Great Work" of transformation is perfected.</note> contains the entire symbolic scene.

Within this frame, two celestial figures stand on either side of a central apparatus. On the left, a figure with a sun for a face holds a quadrant <note>An instrument used for measuring altitudes and navigation, symbolizing the measurement of the heavens.</note>. On the right, a figure with a crescent-moon face holds a similar tool. These figures represent the <term>Duality of Nature</term> <note>The union of the Solar/Masculine/Active and Lunar/Feminine/Passive forces.</note>. Between them sits a triangle topped by a small bird, signifying the spirit or the volatile element rising above the work.

In the heart of the diagram is a large circle containing a six-pointed star, known as the **Seal of Solomon** <note>original: "Signum Salomonis." This star is formed by two overlapping triangles representing the union of Fire and Water.</note>, positioned over stylized flames. From the bottom of this central circle, thick roots extend downward, anchoring these celestial symbols into the ground.

Surrounding the lower half of the circle are five six-pointed stars, each inscribed with a planetary symbol:
*   **Venus** (♀) and the **Moon** (☾) on the left.
*   **Mars** (♂) and **Jupiter** (♃) on the right.
*   **Mercury** (☿) in the bottom center.

The entire scene is supported by a base of stylized, billowing clouds or rocky terrain, representing the <term>First Matter</term> <note>original: "Prima Materia." The formless, base substance from which all things are created.</note> from which the philosophical tree grows.

That certain fruits are found in one place and not in another is due to this cause: the heavens are fixed at two points <note>referring to the celestial poles</note>, as is well known to *astronomers*; around these it revolves. One part cannot reach the other; the stars that are within them are not equal to one another, for there are as many species as there are stars. Because one part cannot reach the other location, it is not possible—due to the curvature of the heavens and the *center* <note>original: "centri"</note> or the midpoint of the earth—that the seed <note>original: "Sahm"</note> of a star from one part could fall upon another part and transport itself there; rather, it must fall where it can, and only straight downwards.

It is indeed true that men carry a herb or a seed to another place and plant it there, where its star cannot reach; it nevertheless grows forth, but this is only due to the inexpressible *multiplication* <note>The alchemical process of increasing the power or quantity of a substance.</note> which is hidden in the spiritual fire, and because the seed receives its proper *digestion* <note>original: "digestion." Secondly, the *digestion* is either too weak or too strong; for a herb that grows in *Arabia* cannot grow in Germany, because its nature requires a strong *digestion*. Likewise, a herb that grows in Germany does not grow in *Arabia*; it is burned therein and comes to nothing. Thus one now sees how the *vegetables* <note>original: "Vegetabilia." # The Unlocked Chest of Nature

give honor to him who has earned it from you and is worthy of it. The Pagans <note>original: "Heyden"</note> relied upon astronomy; they subjected themselves to the stars and held them to be their gods. But you should act and speak as humans, not as the Pagans and the irrational beasts. Let this be said as a warning to you and to everyone, so that God is not provoked to carry out his punishment more severely.

I asked: Is astronomy then nothing, and should it be entirely and completely rejected?

**Response:** Astronomy is in itself a magnificent art, to be praised and held in high esteem if it is used correctly. However, it has become greatly corrupted—as was said before—in that people wish to make gods out of it. Secondly, the correct calculation <note>original: "Calculation"</note> has also been lost, and current astronomers only make do with what Ptolemy <note>Claudius Ptolemy, the 2nd-century Greek astronomer whose geocentric model dominated until the Renaissance</note> described, which is incorrect and false. For now the heavens are different, as they say, and have sunk considerably; they have become slower and lazier in their course, and have also greatly decreased in power. 

Whoever knows the correct method <note>original: "Methodum"</note>, let him use it as a Christian and not like a Pagan: namely, to investigate the time of days and years, and partly also the weather <note>original: "Gewitter," here referring to general atmospheric conditions</note>. But he should not use it as some wish to do: to predict war, rebellion, and other misfortunes, and to present such things as certainties...

For in carbuncles <note>original: "Carfunckeln"; a historic term for deep red gems, usually garnets or rubies</note> and rubies, among all created things, the heavenly fire is found in its purest and most delicate state through a slight preparation, mingled with the soul of the Sun. Within these lies the blossom: pure, delicate, and subtle, scattered like a juice or an expanded matter, and of a much nobler essence than the metal or the seed that is intended to come from it. For in the same way that roses, lavender, spikenard, and other fragrant herbs smell much better and are more lovely while they are still blooming—giving off a much more magnificent scent than when one takes the seed, smells it, or distills it—so too is this blossom much lovelier, more magnificent, and more powerful in strength, might, and virtue than its finished metal.

# The Opened Cabinet of Nature

I asked: Is this "bloom" called the *First Being* <note>original: "primum Ens"; a term used in alchemy to describe the primordial essence of a substance</note> about which Theophrastus <note>Referring to Paracelsus, whose full name was Philippus Aureolus Theophrastus Bombastus von Hohenheim</note> has written many wonders?

He answered, yes: But it is incorrectly named <margin>7.</margin> and understood; for the marcasites, cobalts, etc., are not the *first beings* <note>original: "prima entia"</note>, nor is it that which Theophrastus teaches one to extract *through sublimation and distillation* <note>original: "per sublimationem destillationem"</note>. Rather, it is precisely the beginning of the seed, or the *Ultimate Spiritual Being of Metals or Minerals* <note>original: "Ultimum Ens Spirituale Metallorum vel Mineralium"</note>; that is how it may be titled. But therein lies the *First Being* hidden—that is, the Heavenly *Ethereal* Fire, which nevertheless has its *subject* <note>original: "subjectum"; the physical matter in which the essence resides</note> with it <margin>15.</margin> and is united with it. You must separate these from one another, and not only by *sublimation*, where you only obtain the bloom or flowers of the metals and minerals, breaking them off from their stems, and tearing them out from the weeds—that is, <margin>19.</margin> from the mountains and other impure things. Instead, you must first draw the *First Being* from those very things, in which it lies more easily and is much sooner obtained than from its finished metal. For such a substance is still soft, tender, mild, and spread apart, volatile and pure. In the metal, it is closed together, hard, coarse, and fixed. In the same way (as said before), you can bring out the taste and lovely scent from roses or lavender *through distillation* <note>original: "per destilationem"</note> as a simpler way, than you could also...

When a new <term>conjunction</term> <note>an alignment of celestial bodies believed to trigger earthly events</note> arises in the heavens or the celestial, firmamental <term>stars</term> <note>original: "Astra"</note>, it draws—like an attractive matter (yet acting as a fire toward the Earth)—life from the constellations and the living fire. The awakening of this fire is now transformed into the nature and kind of the firmament, or the form which the firmament has drawn to itself. For instance, if the <term>spiritual body</term> <note>original: "Spiritualische Corpus"</note> is cold, then this <term>soul</term> <note>original: "Anima"</note> or fire also becomes cold in nature. However, if the spiritual body is coarse—having its origin from a coarse star—it also takes to itself matter of its own kind. # Chemical Kabbalah

When silver is transplanted <note>The author uses "transplanted" to describe a chemical or alchemical change in state</note>, it becomes azure <note>Lapis lazuli or a blue pigment</note>; it is not therefore gold, nor another metal, but a particular species, which is nonetheless more of a silvery nature than the property of other metals. Therefore, one should let such gross errors go, for each thing grows for itself; one has nothing to do with the other, just as one herb has nothing to do with another, even though they both come from one matter—namely from the earth—and the minerals from the corporeal water. Thus, the matter <note>original: "Materia"</note> as well as the form are unequal to one another; this should be considered.

I spoke in opposition: It is nonetheless evident that through the Art <note>The "Art" refers to Alchemy</note> one can produce gold and silver from ores that are so lowly and often contain nothing, or only poor metals. From this it is to be concluded that such lowly metals and minerals are transformed and become gold and silver, for one, etc.

Secondly, if the metals were not to transmute into one another, then it would also not be possible for the Art to convert lead, tin, and copper; for the Art is a follower of Nature, and does nothing else, nor can do anything else, than what Nature has modeled for it. Therefore, [if transmutation were impossible], Alchemy would have to be false and unjust, etc.

<margin>70.</margin>

<margin>165.</margin>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the proper refinement of ores, warning against excessive heat and misunderstanding the volatile nature of minerals.</meta>

# The Opened Cabinet of Nature

The same is too greatly mutilated and the flowers <note>In alchemy, "flowers" refers to the volatile sublimate or the purest essence of a metal that rises during heating.</note> are too greatly exposed; rather, you also believe that because there is much material, the *digestion* <note>original: "digestio"; a process of slow, consistent heating to mature a substance.</note> must also be that much greater. Thus, they have made the fire so much larger that in one place the ore was burnt, while in another it felt no fire at all. Therefore, such material has remained a common ore; indeed, at times more harm than benefit resulted from it. Therefore, one should pay close attention to this. 

If, however, an ore—be it as volatile as it wishes (as many believe they can bring forth more from a volatile ore than from a fixed one, which is <margin>52.</margin> rightly said but not rightly understood)—does not carry the flowers of the *Sun* or *Moon* <note>original: "Solis oder Luna"; referring to Gold and Silver.</note> within itself, or receive the same through *addition* <margin>64.</margin> <note>The chemical adding of one substance to another to trigger a reaction.</note>, then one may cook it, roast it, and fry it as one wishes, yet one finds neither *Gold* nor *Silver*, but only the metal to which it is devoted, whose seed and flowers it carries. 

Thus you have the instruction and teaching of the *maturation* <note>The process of "ripening" base metals into noble ones.</note> of ores: that here, art matures them just as nature does—and indeed, does so against and beyond nature. Through the cunning and reason of man, metals are *transmuted* in a way that nature cannot achieve; for nature does not have such hands and feet, and cannot bring together things situated far apart as man can.

<summary>This page cautions alchemists against using excessive heat during digestion and explains that while human art can surpass nature in transmuting metals, it can only extract gold and silver if their "seeds" or "flowers" are already present or added.</summary>

☞ From every **gemstone**, **metal**, and **mineral**, one can make a **salt**; and from that salt, a **plant** or a **tree**—yet each according to its own kind. Thirdly, you can release man from all sickness, make a young man out of an old one, and a healthy man out of a sick one. You can change a man's mind and thoughts, making the most pious man out of the worst rogue. And let it be so: what do you think of such things? Is this not high? Yet it is still so small compared to what follows, for the words of <term>Hermes</term> <note>referring to Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary founder of alchemy</note> are not yet sufficiently explained.

Now listen; we wish also to proceed to the supernatural. This is the key to unlock Heaven and Earth; that is, that you can come into the highest <term>firmament</term> <note>the sphere of the stars</note> of Heaven, into the <term>center of the Earth</term> <note>original: "Centrum Terræ"</note>, and into the depths of the sea. You can see through all mountains, valleys, foliage, grass, animals, men, etc., and in short, see through everything as if you were looking through a glass. You can learn the properties of all things, for everything hidden shall be made manifest to you.

# The Opened Cabinet of Nature

The cause of regeneration is this: because **God** the **Lord**, on account of the terrible Fall of Adam <note>original: "Adæ"</note>, cursed the earth—that is, the elemental, corporeal, and lowest part of the world—and subjected it to corruption. Therefore, neither vegetables, minerals, nor animals can regain their first fortunate state or powerful nature without regeneration, much less reach their lost perfection.

And since philosophical regeneration <note>original: "Regeneratio Philosophica"</note> is nothing other than a purging <note>original: "Purgatio"</note> and separation of the good from the evil, and since the soul <note>original: "Anima"</note> is the highest thing therein—which is life—followed by the spirit <note>original: "Spiritus"</note>, and lastly the body <note>original: "corpus"</note>, which is dead compared to the other two: so must first the soul, as well as the spirit and body, be separated from their perishable nature. Thus, subsequently, these three in the philosophical work <note>original: "opere Philosophico"</note> become pure souls or "fifth essences" <note>term: quintæ essentiæ; the refined quintessence or purest state of a substance</note>. 

For if one gives to the regenerated soul a body—whatever kind of body one desires (provided that the body has a symbolic affinity <note>original: "Symbolū"</note> with that same soul, otherwise anything could come from anything <note>original: "ex qvolibet qvidlibet"</note>)—then the body must follow the soul, and not the soul the body. The reason: in the soul is life, and even more so in the regenerated soul is an everlasting life; but the old body, by itself, is hither-

# The Opened Cabinet of Nature

In the first part, the Spirit of God—which is an unconsuming fire and life itself—hovered and preserved the same. But the abyss of the earth and the darkness—as the most useless, dead part—He abandoned, drawing only the powers of light and life out of the earth into the water. Thus, right at the beginning, a separation occurred through the Spirit of God within this <term>solution</term><note>The dissolving of solid matter into a liquid state, often compared to the "primordial soup" of creation.</note> and <term>putrefaction</term><note>In alchemy, the stage of breaking down or "rotting" matter so it can be reborn into a higher form.</note>. Therefore, the Spirit of God embraced only the water as the uppermost, most powerful, and best part, and enclosed it with His almighty power; however, He left the powerless and dead matter lying in the abyss of darkness. Because the essence and life of all creatures were contained within this solution, the Spirit of God rightfully cherished, covered, strengthened, and ripened it for the increase of His perfect light of life, just as a hen covers her chicks with the wings of her almighty fertility. Now, when everything in this solution and putrefaction was ripened by the Spirit of God and divided into its perfect, actual state of life, the true essential <term>separation</term><note>The alchemical process of isolating pure substances from impure mixtures.</note> followed. Namely, that God, through the command of His almighty Word, first took from this solution that which was most clear, most subtle, most powerful, most highly purified—indeed, all crystalline clarity and radiance—

The surpassing water was separated, and because of its incomprehensible radiance and unspeakable transparency <term>Diaphanitet; from the Greek 'diaphaneia', meaning clarity or translucence</term>, it was named the Light. This is also called the First Water, which possesses the most perfect power and effect among all creatures. Indeed, it is a living spring of all being, from whose flow everything below is refreshed, sustained, and quickened. Therefore, it is also the Form, <margin><note>original: "Actio"</note> Action</margin>, and the essence of all other waters below. Furthermore, it has received the first and highest place nearest to God, through whose influence the heavens, stars, planets, all elements, and <term>inferiora corpora; lower bodies or earthly matter</term> are sustained with their powers and movements.

From this Light, other <term>Separationes; divisions or distillations</term> followed, one higher than the other, which were ordained by God for the creation of the angels, of souls, and also for the preservation of the lower world. This light is called the <term>cœlū Empyreum; the Empyrean Heaven, the highest heaven of pure fire and light</term> or the <term>vivificū; life-giving</term>, as well as the <term>Mundus superior vel invisibilis; the superior or invisible world</term>. Without doubt, Plato had this in mind with his <term>Ideis; Ideas or Archetypes</term>, because in this invisible light the entire visible world is contained, and is governed and sustained by it as the highest creature, according to the will of God.

Following this is the second separation, namely the threefold division—or the three distinctions—of the uppermost, middle, and lowermost waters, which signify the Soul, Spirit, and Body.

# The Opened Cabinet of Nature

Therefore, the *Philosopher* <note>original: "Philosophus"; referring here to the alchemist or natural philosopher</note> must also divide his entire work into four parts, as the main pillars of his *artificial* <note>meaning "artfully constructed" or "alchemical"</note> building: namely, into the Light, the uppermost, the middle, and the lowermost Waters; and he must divide or *separate* these from one another.

The Light is the *Form* <note>original: "Forma"; the shaping principle in Aristotelian philosophy</note>, the living essence and active power, and the burning radiance of the Soul, or the super-celestial, incomprehensible fire.

<margin>Light</margin> The uppermost Water, however, is the *Matter* <note>original: "Materia"</note> or the *Aerial body* of the Soul, its seat and housing, or the imperceptible air. Through the joining and *influence* of these two, a shining, clear, *Crystalline*, super-celestial *Essence*—that is, the *Soul* <note>original: "Anima"</note>—is created.

The middle Water is to be considered a *Form* in relation to the lowermost, but a *Matter* in relation to the uppermost. This is the *Spirit* <note>original: "Spiritus"</note>, which is the body of the Soul. It is the living power, *Form*, and essence of the lowermost *corporeal* water, through which the same must be compelled, purified, and made spiritual. In such a way, the *Spirit* is a living water and the true *Water of Life* <note>original: "Aqua vitae"</note>, in which the uppermost Light with its *Crystalline* water lies. By means of this, the *Body*—that is, the lowermost water—is subsequently *illuminated* and *clarified*, and its previously suppressed, as if dead, life is only now truly awakened and with...

# Preface to the Favorable
## Reader.

Since in this last age of the world and these sorrowful, highly burdensome times, it is unfortunately found in deed and in fact how strictly and poorly the enemy of truth—through his instruments—despises and oppresses with hatred, envy, and mockery <note>original: "Donum hoc Divinum & pretiosissimum"</note> this most precious and divine gift of the truest <term>universal spagyric medicine</term> <note>Spagyric refers to the alchemical process of separating and recombining plant or mineral constituents to create potent medicines.</note>, of the <term>attained art and philosophy</term> <note>original: "artis & Philosophiæ adeptæ"; refers to the "Adept," a master who has successfully discovered the Philosopher's Stone.</note>. In this alone rests and is to be found <note>original: "magnum illud compositum"</note> that great composition of the power and capacity of the upper and lower spheres <note>A reference to the Hermetic principle "as above, so below," suggesting the medicine contains the powers of both the heavens and the earth.</note> in <note>original: "simplicitate"</note> simplicity. It also encloses and comprehends within itself the various qualities of all elements <note>original: "sub unario numero Cabalistico"</note> under the Kabbalistic number of one. Furthermore, in all illnesses and cures, it maintains <note>original: "victoriam & principatum"</note> victory and preeminence over all other medicines, to the honor and praise of **God** who created them. From this alone, the right, pure, most precious, and truest <term>purple potable gold</term> <note>original: "Aurum potabile purpuratum"; a legendary liquid gold remedy believed to cure all diseases and grant longevity.</note> of the new birth and regenerated being—which was called <note>original: "Ambrosia & Nectar"</note> food and drink of the gods by the ancients—has its origin and is possible to obtain. This is for the benefit of the beloved and widely renowned fatherland, in which <note>original: "olim"</note> formerly <note>original: "adepta Philosophia, qvâ naturæ secreta rimantur"</note> the attained philosophy, by which the secrets of nature are investigated, once excellently flourished, and now through **Divine**—

<summary>The author introduces a defense of alchemical and spagyric medicine, lamenting its suppression by enemies of truth while extolling its divine origins and healing power. He frames the "Universal Medicine" as a spiritual and physical restorative linked to the "potable gold" of the ancients.</summary>

## The Third Chapter.

Among all *philosophers* <note>referring here to Alchemists or Natural Philosophers</note>, the *dissolution of gold* <note>original: "solutio auri"</note> is regarded as a great mystery—indeed, even as the *highest secret of the spagyric art* <note>original: "summum arcanum spagiricæ artis"; spagyric refers to the alchemical process of separating and recombining materials</note>. This is not without cause, because nothing more difficult is found in the Sophic work than how it is possible to bring the *fixed* together with the volatile into a proportional *dissolution* <note>original: "solution"</note> and *sublimation* <note>the process of turning a solid into vapor and back to solid to purify it</note>. No one will ever learn this *from reading or the study of the Philosophers by themselves* <note>original: "ex lectione vel Philosophorum studio per se"</note> alone, unless he has discovered it himself through *manual operation and the evidence of his own eyes through manifold experience* <note>original: "Manual operation & oculata fide multiplici experientia"</note>. In such a way, if the *solvent, the potential philosophical key* <note>original: "solvens Philosophischer clavis potentialis"</note>, is to fruitfully *perform* <note>original: "præstiren"</note> its *duty* <note>original: "officium"</note>, and the *acting* and *enduring* <note>original: "agens" and "patiens"; the active and passive principles in a chemical reaction</note> in their penetrating and tinting—

## The Fourth Chapter.

Because in the previous chapter mention was made of the materials through which the true <term>philosophical solution</term> <note>original: "Philosophische solutio"; the process of dissolving a solid into a liquid state</note> is possible, a more extensive report shall now follow regarding what materials such a <term>corrosive</term> <note>original: "Corrosiff"; here referring to a dissolving agent or "menstruum" rather than a simple acid</note> ought to be made from, as current <term>laborers</term> <note>original: "Laboranten"; practical chemists or alchemists</note> stray far and abuse the process in this matter.

It is found in proven experience and natural custom that, just as the dear little grain of wheat does not dissolve in its <term>generating and multiplying essence</term> <note>original: "generirender und multiplicirenden Essenz"</note> except in an external, innate <term>moisture of the earth</term> <note>original: "humido terræ"</note> from which it was first born; when it is cast therein, the <term>body</term> <note>original: "corpus"</note> is dissolved through <term>putrefaction</term> <note>original: "putrefaction"; the stage of decay or blackening necessary for new growth</note>, and the internal moisture of the grain of wheat, along with its sleeping powers of <term>multiplication</term>, is awakened for <term>generation</term>, driven forth, and <term>brought into action</term> <note>original: "ad actum producirt"</note>.

A Cabalistic author and philosopher writes: that there is only one thing in the entire world that dissolves gold and silver. Others write that a special moisture is to be sought in the minerals through wondrous manual operations, which resolves gold without violence. Walburg, Augurellus, Isaac Hollandus <note>A name attributed to one or two Dutch alchemists, likely 15th century</note>—

It emphasizes the principle of "like dissolves like" in the creation of the philosopher's stone or elixir.</summary>

   I introduce this <term>Philosophy</term> <note>Philosophia</note> of the ancients toward the end, before the <term>Conclusion</term> <note>Conclusion</note>, so that whoever wishes to institute and undertake the process of <term>potable gold</term> <note>auri potabilis; a legendary medicinal elixir made from dissolved gold</note> should first reflect well upon himself in such philosophy. Let him consider that it is no common labor, as some so frivolously and quickly presume and undertake to prepare it. Rather, the four <term>Elements</term> <note>Elementa</note> must deliver this work into our hands; *and since the elements are seen to be generated,* <note>original: "& cum Elementa generari videantur"</note> it must further be sought and considered from what they generate themselves, and in what manner they are to be most constantly preserved *under one form and species.* <note>original: "sub una forma & specie"</note> Therefore, a learned author writes that potable gold encompasses two labors within itself. The first is the preparation of the <term>substances</term> <note>species</note> from which it is to be made. The second is the preservation of the same through the <term>Astral Salt</term> <note>Astralisches Saltz; a spiritual or celestial essence in alchemy</note>, which guards the mantle of honor and unites it with the bond of love, so that it becomes fluid and drinkable, and may be further applied and used for other things.

   Thus you have briefly a rich instruction on what the <term>drinkable quality of gold</term> <note>potabile vel potabilitas auri</note> encompasses, from where it arises and originates—not according to the common <term>opinion</term> <note>opinion</note> of the ignorant...

<summary>The author warns that creating potable gold is a profound philosophical and alchemical task requiring the mastery of the four elements and the use of "Astral Salt." He emphasizes that this process is far more complex than common practitioners realize, involving both the preparation and the preservation of the gold's essence.</summary>

<keywords>potable gold, alchemy, four elements, Astral Salt, Platonic Idea, medicinal gold, transmutation</keywords>

--- Page 248 ---
<meta>The previous page likely discussed the false claims of fraudulent alchemists; the catchword "vel" connects to the final word of this page.</meta>

...rants, who deceive themselves and others by claiming that they can make **metallic drinkable and medicinal gold** <note>original: "aurū metallicum potabile & medicinale"</note>; yet they know and have experienced the very least of the **Philosophical Process** and the administration of the **science of Wisdom's Labor** <note>original: "sciencz Sophiæ Laboris"</note>. In this essence and ground of Nature, the substance of gold must first be prepared and created before it is possible to force or reduce it into drinkability, or before it will allow itself to be mastered or handled so that it suffers no damage to its **Mercury, Sulfur, and Salt** <note>The "Tria Prima" or three primary substances of alchemical theory</note> during the solution, nor becomes weakened, adulterated, or altered by foreign additions.

Therefore, dear pious artists, remember what **Petrus Bonus** <note>A 14th-century Italian physician and influential alchemical writer</note> says: **that this liquefaction or drinkability of gold requires long and laborious experience** <note>original: "qvod longam & laboriosam experientiam reqvirat hæc auri liqvefactio vel potabilitas"</note>. Consequently, undertake nothing in this matter except what may happen through the power of Nature, attending to nothing contrary to her; thus you shall hit upon the **medium** <note>The middle state or mediating substance</note> that exists between the last and the first.

For in this way you remain within the bounds and **steps of Nature**, out of which the **"Increase and multiply"** <note>original: "Crescite & multiplicamini"; a reference to the biblical command in Genesis, applied here to the growth of minerals</note> arises. Because these words were spoken by God during the **first creation**, Nature must maintain her course and remain within such limits, outside of which no essences, flowers, or...

<summary>The author warns against fraudulent alchemists and emphasizes that creating "potable gold" requires a deep understanding of the natural laws and the three primary substances (Mercury, Sulfur, and Salt). He cites the authority of Petrus Bonus to argue that true alchemy must follow the divine creative command to "increase and multiply" within Nature's own limits.</summary>
<keywords>potable gold, alchemy, Petrus Bonus, Tria Prima, Mercury, Sulfur, Salt, natural law, divine creation</keywords>

--- Page 249 ---
<meta>This page concludes the discourse on the stability of metallic tinctures, following the previous discussion on the preparation of gold.</meta>

...or the colors of gold can never be maintained in its potability <note>original: "Potabilität"; refers to "potable gold," a medicinal liquid form of the metal</note>, oiliness <note>original: "Olität"</note>, or liquidity. For Nature is true, as are her principles in the progress of her ripening and preparation; but the artist is seldom true in his <margin>+</margin> administration of her. Hence these tears <note>Latin: "Hinc illæ lachrymæ"; a classical idiom meaning "that is the cause of the trouble."</note>. Therefore, remain within the predestined essence of Nature, so that you may bring the quadrangle of the universal into the roundness and points of the circle <note>An alchemical metaphor for squaring the circle, or achieving perfection and unity in the Great Work</note>. Then, to the honor and praise of God, one invincible truth shall give birth to another; to which end may the gracious God grant His blessing and grace to you and to us.

->T H E  E N D<-

<summary>The author concludes the treatise by warning that failures in alchemy stem from the artist's errors rather than Nature's laws, ending with a mystical exhortation to seek divine truth through the symbolic squaring of the circle.</summary>

## The First,

Every person desires by nature
to possess great golden and silver treasures, noble
stones, and riches, and
to be great and high before the world. God has
also created all of this so that man
should use it, be a master over it, and thereby recognize,
praise, honor, and thank Him for His divine
goodness and omnipotence. But
everyone wants to find these things only during good days, without
the effort, danger, and labor where God has
placed them; they wish to have them sought out, and
do not want to dig, search, or find for themselves,
because for a long time now the
place and the way to them has been unknown and
hidden from the <term>great multitude</term> <note>original: "meisten Hauffen," referring to the uninitiated masses</note>. For to
attain them is difficult and arduous, as it is dangerous,

but yet not impossible to attain. However, since God wishes nothing to be hidden from His great multitude, but rather—in this final century <note>original: "seculo"; referring to the belief that the world was in its final age before the Apocalypse</note> and before the final Judgment breaks forth—it must be revealed to the worthy, as Christ Himself speaks (though very obscurely so that the unworthy may not understand it) when He says: *nothing must remain hidden.* Thus, we are driven by the Spirit of God to proclaim this will of the Lord to the world, just as it has been issued and published by us in various languages. 

But it is either taken poorly by the great multitude, or indeed despised, or sought and desired from us without God. For they imagine that one should teach them the Art (to cook gold in the Alchemical manner <term>Alchemical manner: the chemical process of transmutation</term>), or that we should run to meet them with great treasures—however much they desire these for their splendor, pride, war, usury, gluttony, drunkenness, unchastity, or other sins—and share our own with them. 

Yet they should take an example from the ten virgins <note>A reference to the Parable of the Ten Virgins in Matthew 25</note>, where the five foolish ones demanded oil from the five wise ones; it must be attained in a much different way, through each person's own effort in and through God. **I** step into the seven circles of heaven; I grasp the uppermost with my thoughts and stand with my feet upon the lowest circle. Where the moonlight dazzles my eyes and I stumble or fall, and break a leg, I simply fashion a stilt and walk slowly; that is a balsam to my internal salt <note>In alchemy, "Salt" represents the physical body or the principle of fixity</note> that heals me again. But if I become sweating, a lovely sweet water comes out of my <term>pores</term> <note>original: "poris"</note>, like milk and honey; then I burn my stilts into pure ash. When the ash glows without smoke, the King’s fountain gives forth marigolds, and then three fall into the well and create a darkness of the world until the moonlight breaks through again and becomes clear. The night has passed, the sun gives its shine, the days of the Lord approach; the heaven becomes pure fire and ignites the whole world. All four <term>elements</term> <note>original: "Elementa" - Earth, Air, Fire, and Water</note> melt away, and a new heaven and earth are born. In these short words is the treasure of the world.
